Applications of (higher) categorical trace II: Deligne-Lusztig theory
D. Gaitsgory, N. Rozenblyum and Y. Varshavsky

Abstract
. We use the formalism of the (2-category) AGCat \operatorname{AGCat} , developed in [ GRV ] , and the operation of higher categorical trace to (re)derive a number of results in the Deligne-Lusztig theory.



Introduction

0.1.
What is this paper about?

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over the ground field
k
. For the applications in this paper we take
k=\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}
,
the algebraic closure of a finite field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

0.1.1.

In
[
GRV
]
we introduced a (symmetric monoidal)
(\infty,2)
-category
\operatorname{AGCat}
, which is a version
of the (usual)
(\infty,2)
-category
\operatorname{DGCat}
of DG categories (with coefficients
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
) that is
better compatible with the theory of
\ell
-adic sheaves.

Namely, the assignment

(X\in\operatorname{Sch})\mapsto(\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X)\in\operatorname{AGCat})

is
strictly
symmetric monoidal, i.e., the functor

(0.1)

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X_{1})\otimes\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X_{2})\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(X_{1}\times X_{2})

is an equivalence (by design). This is in contrast with

(X\in\operatorname{Sch})\mapsto(\operatorname{Shv}(X)\in\operatorname{DGCat}),

for which the functor

\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1})\otimes\operatorname{Shv}(X_{2})\to\operatorname{Shv}(X_{1}\times X_{2})

is only fully faithful (and it never an equivalence unless one of the schemes is a union of points).

0.1.2.

The category
\operatorname{AGCat}
is a formal device (its definition has no algebro-geometric or categorical content).
Yet, it is useful.

In this paper, we will apply
\operatorname{AGCat}
to develop a theory of
categorical representations of
G
and to give conceptual proofs of a number of results about character sheaves and
Deligne-Lusztig functors. We establish the independence of the Deligne-Lusztig functor of the parabolic
at the level characters

(which seems to have been known in full generality).

0.1.3.

Here is how
\operatorname{AGCat}
is used in this paper:

Given an algebraic group
G
, we define a 2-category
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
of
categorical representations of
G
,
where by a categorical representation we will mean one on an object of
\operatorname{AGCat}
(rather than a more naive notion,
on which the action would be on an object of
\operatorname{DGCat}
).

A typical example of an object of
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
is
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathcal{Y}})
, where
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a scheme (or, more generally, an arbitrary prestack)
acted on by
G
.

0.2.
Categorical representations: general theory

0.2.1.

The fact that (
0.1
) is an equivalence implies that
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
is a very well-behaved object.
Here are the three main tools that we will use to develop the theory:

•

The fact that for an endomorphism
\phi
of
G
, the (
2
-categorical) trace
\operatorname{Tr}(\phi,G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\in\operatorname{AGCat}

identifies with
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}_{\phi}(G))
, where
\operatorname{Ad}_{\phi}(G)
refers the action of
G
on itself by

\phi
-twisted conjugacy. The two main cases of interest are when
\phi=\operatorname{id}
and
\phi={\operatorname{Frob}}
;

•

The induction functor

{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}:M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\to G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod},

where
G
is reductive,
P\subset G
is a parabolic, and
M
is the Levi quotient of
P
;

•

For a pair
(P_{1},P_{2})
of
associated parabolics
, a natural transformation

(0.2)

{\mathbf{ind}}_{P_{1}}\overset{I_{P_{1},P_{2}}}{\to}{\mathbf{ind}}_{P_{2}},

which we call
the intertwining functor
.

These three pieces of structure combine to produce some interesting, albeit well-known, constructions
in geometric representation theory.

0.2.2.

For
\phi=\operatorname{id}
, we have

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G));

there is no surprise here.

Now, we can consider

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}):\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\to\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}).

Thus, we obtain a functor

(0.3)

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(M/\operatorname{Ad}(M))\simeq\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\overset{\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathbf{ind}}_{P})}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)).

We show that (
0.3
) identifies with the
Grothendieck-Springer
functor

\operatorname{GrothSpr}_{P}:\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(M/\operatorname{Ad}(M))\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)),

i.e., the functor given by pull-push along the diagram

(0.4)

\begin{CD}P/\operatorname{Ad}(P)@>{}>{}>G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)\\
@V{}V{}V\\
M/\operatorname{Ad}(M).\end{CD}

0.2.3.

For
\phi={\operatorname{Frob}}
, by Lang’s theorem, we can identify

G/\operatorname{Ad}_{\operatorname{Frob}}(G)\simeq\operatorname{pt}/G({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),

and hence

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}_{\operatorname{Frob}}(G))\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(\operatorname{pt}/G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})).

Note now that the functor

(0.5)

{\mathbf{i}}(\operatorname{Shv}(\operatorname{pt}/G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})))\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(\operatorname{pt}/G({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))

is an equivalence (see Lemma
3.1.9
), where
{\mathbf{i}}
is the tautological embeddings
\operatorname{DGCat}\to\operatorname{DGCat}

(see
[
GRV
, Sect. 2.4.1]
).

Hence,

(0.6)

\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}_{\operatorname{Frob}}(G))\simeq\underline{{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),

where

\underline{{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})):={\mathbf{i}}({\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))).

0.2.4.

We consider the “double trace” isomorphism

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\simeq\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})),

which expands to

(0.7)

\mathsf{Funct}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})/\operatorname{Ad}(G({\mathcal{F}}_{q})),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})\simeq\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\simeq\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}))\simeq\\
\simeq\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}))\simeq\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))\simeq\mathsf{Funct}((G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}),

and we show that it equals the tautological identification.

0.2.5.

For a Levi subgroup
M
defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and a parabolic

M\hookrightarrow P\twoheadrightarrow M

(but
P
is not necessarily defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
), the natural transformation

I_{P,{\operatorname{Frob}}(P)}
gives rise to a natural transformation

{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}\circ{\operatorname{Frob}}_{M}\to{\operatorname{Frob}}_{G}\circ{\mathbf{ind}}_{P},

and hence to a functor

\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}):\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{M},M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\to\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{G},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}).

Thus we obtain a functor

(0.8)

\underline{{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}}(M({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\simeq\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{M},M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\overset{\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},{\mathbf{ind}}_{P})}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{G},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\simeq\underline{{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})).

We identify (
0.8
) with the functor, induced by the Deligne-Lusztig functor introduced in
[
DL
,
Lu1
]
(see also
[
DM
]
)

\operatorname{DL}_{P}:{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}(M({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})).

0.2.6.

However, this is as far as we could take the theory with the above tools.

0.3.
Categorical representations: the spectrally finite subcategory

Now, the theory gets richer once we replace the entire
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}

by its full subcategory that we denote by

G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\subset G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod};

we refer the reader to Sect.
0.6
for the definition and to
Sect.
5.1
for complete details.

0.3.1.

We show that the (full) subcategory

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}})\subset\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})

identifies with the subcategory of
character sheaves

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}_{\operatorname{Frob}}(G))\subset\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}_{\operatorname{Frob}}(G)).

This essentially a paraphrase of a result of D. Ben-Zvi and D. Nadler.

0.3.2.

We also show that the (full) subcategory

\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}})\subset\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})

is
entire category

\underline{{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
; this a paraphrase of a result of A. Eteve.

0.3.3.

We use this to show that the “double trace” isomorphism (
0.7
) restricts to an isomorphism

\mathsf{Funct}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})/\operatorname{Ad}(G({\mathcal{F}}_{q})),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})\simeq\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm Rep}}}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\simeq\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}))\simeq\\
\simeq\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}})),

leading to the conclusion that

(0.9)

\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},\operatorname{Shv}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)))\simeq\mathsf{Funct}((G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}).

Note that in the above formula, we are considering the object
\operatorname{Shv}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))\in\operatorname{DGCat}
rather than

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))\in\operatorname{AGCat}
. One can view (
0.9
) as a counterpart for character
sheaves of the Trace Conjecture from
[
AGKRRV1
]
.

Remark 0.3.4
.

The passage

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))\rightsquigarrow\operatorname{Shv}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))

above is part of the general
phenomenon: as we shall see in Theorem
5.1.10
, the passage

G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\rightsquigarrow G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}

gets rid of the extra “algebro-geometric” directions inherent to objects of
\operatorname{AGCat}
, see Sect.
0.6.6
.

0.3.5.

The functor
{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}
restricts to a functor

{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}:M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\to G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}},

and the natural transformation
I_{P_{1},P_{2}}
restricts to a natural transformation
restricts to a natural transformation

(0.10)

{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P_{1}}\overset{I^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P_{1},P_{2}}}{\to}{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P_{2}}.

Here is, however, the fact that really distinguishes the spectrally finite situation from the
general one: the natural transformation (
0.10
) is an isomorphism.

This implies, among the rest, that
I^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P_{1},P_{2}}
induces an isomorphism
between the Grothendieck-Springer functors

\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{1}}:\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(M/\operatorname{Ad}(M))\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))

and

\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{2}}:\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(M/\operatorname{Ad}(M))\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)),

to be denoted

(0.11)

\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{1}}\overset{i_{P_{1},P_{2}}}{\simeq}\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{2}}.

We will refer to (
0.11
) as the
functional equation
for the Grothendieck-Springer functors.
We perceive it as an analog of the functional equation for geometric Eisenstein series in the geometric
Langlands theory, see
[
BG
]
.

Remark 0.3.6
.

As was shown by S. Gunningham, an analog of the functional equation (
0.11
) would be
false

if we did not restrict ourselves to character sheaves.

0.3.7.

A naive attempt to extend the functional equation to the Deligne-Lustig functor fails (for some obvious reasons,
see Remark
3.4.11
). And indeed, it cannot hold, since it is known that
\operatorname{DL}_{P}
, viewed as
a functor,
does
depend on the choice of
P
.

However, it was conjectured in
[
Lu1
]
and established in many cases in
[
DL
,
Lu2
,
BM
]
(see a summary in
[
Lu3
, 0.1]
or
[
DM
, Chapter 9]
) that the map

(0.12)

\operatorname{ch}(\operatorname{DL}_{P}):\mathsf{Funct}((M/\operatorname{Ad}(M))({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to\mathsf{Funct}((G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})

is
independent
of the choice of
P
.

This independence is one of the two main new results of this paper; it appears as Theorem
8.7.2
. We outline
the proof in the next subsection.

0.4.
Independence of Deligne-Lusztig characters of the parabolic

0.4.1.

The first step in the proof is the double trace isomorphism, but applied to
to the functor

{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}
.

First, using the intertwining functor
I_{P,{\operatorname{Frob}}(P)}
we construct an isomorphism

{\operatorname{Frob}}_{G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)}\circ\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P}\simeq\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P}\circ{\operatorname{Frob}}_{M/\operatorname{Ad}(M)},

to be denoted
\beta_{P}
. This identification induces a map

\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{M/\operatorname{Ad}(M)},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(M/\operatorname{Ad}(M)))\to\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))),

to be denoted
\operatorname{Tr}(\beta_{P},\operatorname{GrothSpr}_{P})
.

The double trace isomorphism says that the map (
0.12
) equals the map

(0.13)

\mathsf{Funct}((M/\operatorname{Ad}(M))({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\overset{\text{\eqref{e:Tr isom Intro}}}{\simeq}\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{M/\operatorname{Ad}(M)},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(M/\operatorname{Ad}(M)))\overset{\operatorname{Tr}(\beta_{P},\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P})}{\longrightarrow}\\
\simeq\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}}_{G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)},\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)))\overset{\text{\eqref{e:Tr isom Intro}}}{\simeq}\mathsf{Funct}((G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))({\mathbb{F}}_{q})).

This assertion is the second main new result of this paper; we state it as Corollary
8.4.8
.

Remark 0.4.2
.

The equality of (
0.12
) and (
0.13
) for
q\gg 0
is the main result of
[
Lu3
]
; it is also conjectured in

loc.cit.
that the assumption that
q\gg 0
can be removed.

A particular case of the equality of (
0.12
) and (
0.13
) is the following statement established in

[
Lu2
]
:

Let
M=T
be a torus, and let
\chi
be a 1-dimensional character sheaf on
T
equipped with a Weil structure.
On the one hand, we can form the Grothendieck-Springer sheaf
\operatorname{GrothSpr}_{B}(\chi)
. We equip it with a Weil
structure, by first defining on the regular semi-simple locus of
G
(using the
W
-action and the Weil structure on
\chi
),
and then applying the Goresky-MacPherson extension.

On the one hand, we consider the function

\mathsf{funct}(\operatorname{GrothSpr}_{B}(\chi))\in\mathsf{Funct}((G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),

obtained by taking pointwise traces of Frobenius on
\operatorname{GrothSpr}_{B}(\chi)
with respect
to the above Weil structure.

On the other hand, we consider the function

\operatorname{ch}(\operatorname{DL}_{B}(\chi^{0}))\in\mathsf{Funct}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})/\operatorname{Ad}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}).

where
\chi^{0}
is the character of
T({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, obtained by taking pointwise traces of Frobenius on
\chi

(i.e.,
\chi^{0}=\mathsf{funct}(\chi)
).

Then the equality of (
0.12
) and (
0.13
) in particular implies that

(0.14)

\mathsf{funct}((\operatorname{GrothSpr}_{B}(\chi))=\operatorname{ch}(\operatorname{DL}_{B}(\chi^{0}))

as elements of

\mathsf{Funct}((G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})=\mathsf{Funct}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})/\operatorname{Ad}(G({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}).

Note that the usual proof of (
0.14
) (see, e.g.,
[
Laum
, Theorem 2.3.1]
, which follows
[
Lu2
]
) uses
some non-trivial computations involving Jordan decomposition.
By contrast, our proof is a diagram chase (once we accept the formula for local terms in the Grothendieck-Lefschetz fixed point
theorem, as generalized in
[
GaVa
]
).

0.4.3.

Thus, in order to show that the map (
0.12
) is independent of
P
, it suffices to show that the map (
0.13
) is.

Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be a pair of parabolics containing
M
. We already know that thanks to the functional equation
(
0.11
), the functors
\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{1}}
and
\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{2}}
are
isomorphic.

Therefore, in order to show that the maps (
0.12
) for
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are equal, it suffices to show that

i_{P_{1},P_{2}}
intertwines the isomorphisms
\beta_{P_{1}}
and
\beta_{P_{2}}
, i.e., that the diagram

\begin{CD}{\operatorname{Frob}}_{G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)}\circ\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{1}}@>{\beta_{P_{1}}}>{}>\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{1}}\circ{\operatorname{Frob}}_{M/\operatorname{Ad}(M)}\\
@V{i_{P_{1},P_{2}}}V{}V@V{}V{i_{P_{1},P_{2}}}V\\
{\operatorname{Frob}}_{G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)}\circ\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{2}}@>{\beta_{P_{2}}}>{}>\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{2}}\circ{\operatorname{Frob}}_{M/\operatorname{Ad}(M)}\end{CD}

commutes.

Unwinding the construction of
\beta_{P}
, we obtain that we have prove the following statement, which does not involve the Frobenius map:

For a triple of parabolics
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
containing
M
, we have

(0.15)

i_{P_{2},P_{3}}\circ i_{P_{1},P_{2}}\simeq i_{P_{1},P_{3}}

as natural transformations

\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{1}}\to\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{3}}.

The fact that (
0.15
) holds is our Theorem
7.1.2
. Let us comment on how it is proved.

0.4.4.

First, there is the easy case, namely, when the relative positions of
(P_{1},P_{2})
and
(P_{2},P_{3})
add up.
In this case, we have

(0.16)

I_{P_{2},P_{3}}\circ I_{P_{1},P_{2}}\simeq I_{P_{1},P_{3}}

as natural transformations

{\mathbf{ind}}_{P_{1}}\to{\mathbf{ind}}_{P_{3}},

and (
0.15
) is obtained by taking
\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},-)
on (
0.16
).

0.4.5.

Thus, by induction on the distance between
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, we reduce the verification of (
0.15
) to the case when

P_{3}=P_{1}
. I.e., we need to show that

(0.17)

i_{P_{2},P_{1}}\circ i_{P_{1},P_{2}}\simeq\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P_{1}}}.

Note that it is
not at all
true that

(0.18)

I_{P_{2},P_{1}}\circ I_{P_{1},P_{2}}

is the identity endomorphism of
{\mathbf{ind}}_{P_{1}}
. In fact, this endomorphism is closely related to the Serre functor,
see Sect.
0.5.5
.

Yet, it so happens that the non-triviality of (
0.18
) gets swallowed by the operation
\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},-)
.
We prove this in Theorem
7.1.6
.

0.4.6.

We find it curious that manipulations involving a 3-category (in our case
2\operatorname{-AGCat}
) can be used to prove a non-trivial
equality of (
\ell
-adic) numbers (which is what the independence of (
0.12
) of the parabolic is). The proof essentially boils
down to checking commutativity of huge diagrams, but after packaging them so as to make it humanly possible.

At its most complex, one needs to show that two certain 3-morphisms (in a 3-category) are equal. These 3-morphisms arise
by taking units and counits of adjunctions of some 1-morphisms (these are 2-morphisms), then taking
their

adjoints, and considering 3-morphisms that are units and counits of these adjunctions. An example of this
is Corollary
1.8.7
, which stands behind the equality of (
0.12
) and
(
0.13
).

A related strategy, but one that occurs at one categorical level lower (i.e., one works with the 2-category
\operatorname{DGCat}

rather than with the 3-category
2\operatorname{-AGCat}
) was realized in
[
KP
]
for the proof of the Riemann-Roch theorem.

0.5.
What else is done in this paper?

We will now explain how the 3-categorical perspective allows one to reprove a number
of results about character sheaves.

0.5.1.

Consider again the functor

{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}:M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\to G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}.

It admits a right adjoint, called the Jacquet functor

M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\leftarrow G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}:\mathbf{jacq}_{P}.

Moreover, this adjunction has the following property: the unit and counit natural transformations

(0.19)

\operatorname{unit}:\operatorname{Id}_{M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}}\to\mathbf{jacq}_{P}\circ{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}\text{ and }\operatorname{counit}:{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}\circ\mathbf{jacq}_{P}\to\operatorname{Id}_{G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}}

themselves admit right adjoints.

Passing to these right adjoints, we obtain the natural transformations

\operatorname{unit}^{R}:\mathbf{jacq}_{P}\circ{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}\to\operatorname{Id}_{M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}}\text{ and }\operatorname{counit}^{R}:\operatorname{Id}_{G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}}\to{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}\circ\mathbf{jacq}_{P},

which make
\mathbf{jacq}_{P}
into a
left
adjoint of
{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}
.

Thus, we obtain that the
({\mathbf{ind}}_{P},\mathbf{jacq}_{P})
-adjunction is
ambidexterous
.

0.5.2.

However, an even more interesting thing happens when we pass to the spec.fin subcategries. In this case, the natural transformations

(0.20)

\operatorname{unit}^{R}:\mathbf{jacq}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}\circ{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}\to\operatorname{Id}_{M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}}

and

(0.21)

\operatorname{counit}^{R}:\operatorname{Id}_{G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}}\to{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}\circ\mathbf{jacq}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}

themselves
admit right adjoints.

0.5.3.

The existence of these right adjoints has a consequence for the Grothendieck-Springer functor.
Let

{\mathfrak{r}}_{P}:\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(M/\operatorname{Ad}(M))

be the right adjoint of
\operatorname{GrothSpr}_{P}
. Explicitly,
{\mathfrak{r}}_{P}
is obtained by pull-push along the diagram transpose to (
0.4
).

Denote

{\mathfrak{r}}_{P}^{\operatorname{ch}}:={\mathfrak{r}}_{P}|_{\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))}.

The fact that the natural transformations
(
0.20
) and (
0.21
) admit right adjoints implies that

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},\mathbf{jacq}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P})\simeq{\mathfrak{r}}_{P}^{\operatorname{ch}}

is a
left
adjoint of

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P})=\operatorname{GrothSpr}_{P}^{\operatorname{ch}}.

I.e., the
(\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P},{\mathfrak{r}}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P})
-adjunction is also ambidexterous.

Using the fact that the functor
\operatorname{GrothSpr}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P}
commutes with Verdier duality, the above implies that
the functor
{\mathfrak{r}}^{\operatorname{ch}}_{P}
also commutes with Verdier duality. The latter fact was, however, known thanks to

[
CY
,
GIT
]
.

0.5.4.

Let us return to the natural transformations (
0.20
) and (
0.21
). By passing to
their

right adjoints, we obtain
another
adjunction

(0.22)

{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}\leftrightarrow\mathbf{jacq}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}.

This adjunction differs from the original
({\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P},\mathbf{jacq}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P})
-adjunction by
an automorphism of
{\mathbf{ind}}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P}
, which we call the
Serre functor
. Effectively, the Serre
functor is an endomorphism of

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/N(P))^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\in(G\times M)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod};

we denote it by
\operatorname{Se}_{G/N(P)}
.

0.5.5.

Our Theorem
5.8.4
identifies
\operatorname{Se}_{G/N(P)}
explicitly. Namely, it says that

(0.23)

\operatorname{Se}_{G/N(P)}\simeq I^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P^{-},P}\circ I^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}_{P,P^{-}},

where
P^{-}
is the opposite parabolic.

0.5.6.

We use (
0.23
) to give an alternative prove of the fact (also established in
[
CY
,
GIT
]
) that
the Harish-Chandra transform

\operatorname{Shv}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))\overset{\operatorname{HC}_{P}}{\to}\operatorname{Shv}((N(P)\backslash G/N(P))/\operatorname{Ad}(M)),

followed by the intertwining functor

\operatorname{Id}\otimes I^{!}_{P,P^{-}}:\operatorname{Shv}((N(P)\backslash G/N(P))/\operatorname{Ad}(M))\to\operatorname{Shv}((N(P)\backslash G/N(P^{-}))/\operatorname{Ad}(M))

also commutes with Verdier duality, when restricted to

\operatorname{Shv}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))\subset\operatorname{Shv}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G)),

see Corollary
7.5.14
.

In the above formulas,
\operatorname{Id}\otimes\operatorname{HC}_{P}
is given by !-pull and *-push along the diagram

\begin{CD}G/\operatorname{Ad}(P)@>{}>{}>(N(P)\backslash G/N(P))/\operatorname{Ad}(M)\\
@V{}V{}V\\
G/\operatorname{Ad}(G),\end{CD}

and
I^{!}_{P,P^{-}}
is given by *-pull and !-push along the diagram

\begin{CD}(N(P)\backslash G)/\operatorname{Ad}(M)@>{}>{}>(N(P)\backslash G/N(P^{-}))/\operatorname{Ad}(M)\\
@V{}V{}V\\
(N(P)\backslash G/N(P))/\operatorname{Ad}(M).\end{CD}

0.6.
Monodromicity and restricted categorical representations

As was mentioned above, the more interesting results of this paper require that we work with the
subcategory

G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\subset G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}.

We will now explain its definition.

0.6.1.

Let us first take
G=T
to be the torus. We shall say that an an object
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}\in T\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}

is
monodromic
if it is generated by its
(T,\chi)
-invariants, where
\chi
runs over
all possible character sheaves on
T
.

We let

T\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\subset T\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}

consist of monodromic objects.

0.6.2.

For a general reductive
G
, we let
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}
to be the full subcategory consisting of objects
for which

\mathbf{inv}_{N}(\underline{{\mathbf{C}}})\in T\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}

is monodromic. In the above formula,
\mathbf{inv}_{N}
is the functor of invariants with respect to
N\subset B
.

0.6.3.
Example

Consder

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/B)\in G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}.

This object belongs to
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}
.

Consider now

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G/N)\in G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}.

This object does
not
belong to
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}
.

0.6.4.

Of course, one needs to prove something in order to make the above definition useful. The key fact is that the functor

\mathbf{inv}_{N}:G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\to\operatorname{AGCat},

which naturally upgrades to a functor

(0.24)

\mathbf{inv}_{N}^{\operatorname{enh}}:G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\to\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}(\operatorname{AGCat}),\quad\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}:=\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(N\backslash G/N),

with the latter functor being an equivalence, according to a theorem of
[
BGO
]
(reproved in the present paper as Theorem
5.2.4
).

Thus, the knowledge of
\mathbf{inv}_{N}(\underline{{\mathbf{C}}})
(equipped with an action of
\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}
) fully recovers
\underline{{\mathbf{C}}}
.

0.6.5.

Let

\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\operatorname{mon}}\subset\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}

be the full subcategory that consists of
T
-monodromic objects (with respect to the action of
T
on
N\backslash G/N
from
the left/right, or equivalently, both sides). The above embedding admits a right adjoint. Moreover, the object
\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\operatorname{mon}}\in\operatorname{AGCat}

carries a unique structure associative algebra for which the above right adjoint

\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}\to\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\operatorname{mon}}

is a homomorphism.

We can identify

G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\simeq\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\operatorname{mon}}\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}(\operatorname{AGCat}).

0.6.6.

One of the key points of the spec.fin theory is that it effectively takes place in the usual world of DG categories,
rather than
\operatorname{AGCat}
:

Let

{\mathbf{i}}:\operatorname{DGCat}\to\operatorname{AGCat}

be the tautological embedding (see
[
GRV
, Sect. 2.4.1]
). The point is
that
\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\operatorname{mon}}
(unlike
\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}
!) lies in the essential image of this functor, i.e.,

\underline{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\operatorname{mon}}={\mathbf{i}}({\mathcal{H}}^{\operatorname{mon}}),\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{\operatorname{mon}}:=\operatorname{Shv}(N\backslash G/N)^{\operatorname{mon}}.

Set

G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{restr}}:={\mathcal{H}}^{\operatorname{mon}}{\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{DGCat})\in 2\operatorname{-DGCat}.

We obtain that

(0.25)

G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\simeq\operatorname{AGCat}\underset{\operatorname{DGCat}}{\otimes}G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{restr}}.

I.e.,
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}
is the extension of scalars of a 2-category that is a module over
the usual
\operatorname{DGCat}
.

This implies among other things that

(0.26)

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))\simeq{\mathbf{i}}(\operatorname{Shv}^{\operatorname{ch}}(G/\operatorname{Ad}(G))).

0.6.7.

The equivalence (
0.25
) means that the entire restricted/spectrally finite theory
can be rewritten without ever mentioning
\operatorname{AGCat}
: one can just work with the 2-category

{\mathcal{H}}^{\operatorname{mon}}{\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{DGCat}),

and one can transcribe all the proofs in its terms.

The advantage of using
\operatorname{AGCat}
is that it gives a clearer geometric picture, which suggests
natural proofs of many results.

A vague analogy is:
to think about representations of a p-adic group as such vs as a collection of modules for
Hecke algebras.

0.7.
The hidden motivation for writing this paper

One of the key motivations for writing this paper is that it is supposed to be a
prelude to the case
when instead of the usual reductive group
G
, one considers its loop group
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
.

0.7.1.

One can define the object

{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\in 2\operatorname{-DGCat}

in a way completely parallel to
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
.

We have a well-defined object

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/\operatorname{Ad}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\in\operatorname{AGCat}

and an identification

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/\operatorname{Ad}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)).

0.7.2.

Note, however, that Lang’s theorem fails for
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)
. The quotient

{\mathfrak{L}}(F)/\operatorname{Ad}_{\operatorname{Frob}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))=:\operatorname{Isoc}_{G}

is the stack of isocrystals, extensively studied recently by X. Zhu in
[
Zhu
]
.
One can think of
\operatorname{Isoc}_{G}
as an algebro-geometric incarnation of the Fargues-Scholze
\operatorname{Bun}_{G}(\operatorname{FFCurve})
.

As in (
0.6
), we have

\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathfrak{L}}(F)/\operatorname{Ad}_{\operatorname{Frob}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)))=\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(\operatorname{Isoc}_{G}).

0.7.3.

Here are some basic questions that one wants to ask in the loop case:

(1)

Is it true that the loop analog of (
0.5
), i.e., the functor

{\mathbf{i}}(\operatorname{Shv}(\operatorname{Isoc}_{G}))\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(\operatorname{Isoc}_{G}),

is an equivalence?

(2)

What is the subcategory
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\subset{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
, so that

{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\simeq\operatorname{AGCat}\underset{\operatorname{DGCat}}{\otimes}{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{restr}}

for
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{restr}}\in 2\operatorname{-DGCat}
?

(3)

Is it true that an analog of Eteve’s theorem holds, i.e., the (fully faithful) functor

\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}})\to\operatorname{Tr}({\operatorname{Frob}},{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(\operatorname{Isoc}_{G})

is an equivalence?

(4)

What is the definition of

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/\operatorname{Ad}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))\subset\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/\operatorname{Ad}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))?

(5)

Is it true that

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}})=\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/\operatorname{Ad}({\mathfrak{L}}(G))

as subcategories of

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod})\simeq\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/\operatorname{Ad}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)),

respectively.

0.7.4.

Here are some tentative answers:

First, it seems that Question (1) can be settled affirmatively using the results of
[
Zhu
]
.

Question (2) is far more complicated than in the finite-dimensional case, because we do not have
anything resembling the equivalence of (
0.24
).

Rather, a promising approach seems to characterize
{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}
using a (geometric analog)
of the Fintzen-Yu classification (or its refinement, recently found by
[
DVY
]
).

As to Question (3), it does seem plausible that the method of proof of Eteve’s theorem (see Theorem
8.1.3
)
is applicable and gives the desired result.

Finally, for Questions (4) and (5) the best we can do is cheat and
define

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}^{\operatorname{ch}}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)/\operatorname{Ad}({\mathfrak{L}}(G)):=\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathfrak{L}}(G)\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}).

0.8.
Structure of the paper

We now briefly describe the contents of this paper section-by-section.

0.8.1.

In Sect.
1
we explain how the formalism of trace plays out in the 2- and 3-categorical settings:

In the case of 1-categories, given a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{O}}
, a dualizable object
{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}
and an
endomorphism
F
of
{\mathbf{O}}
, one attaches to it an endomorphism

\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{o}})
of the unit object
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}
.

This gets more interesting for 2-categories: given two pairs
({\mathbf{o}}_{1},F_{1})
and
({\mathbf{o}}_{2},F_{2})
as above and a 1-morphism

t:{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
that
weakly intertwines
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
, i.e, we have a natural transformation

t\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha}{\to}F_{2}\circ t,

we obtain a map

\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha,t):\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{1})\to\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2},{\mathbf{o}}_{2}),

provided that
t
is adjointable
1
1

1

In this paper “adjointable” means “admits a right adjoint”.
, a notion that makes
sense for a 1-morphism in a 2-category.

And this gets even richer (and more mind-twisting) for 3-categories.

This section summarizes the results about trace manipulations that we will apply in later sections in
representation-theoretic situations.

0.8.2.

In Sect.
2
we introduce the notion of categorical representation of an algebraic group. These
will form an object, denoted
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
in the ambient 3-category that we denote
2\operatorname{-AGCat}

(morally, 2-categories, tensored over
\operatorname{AGCat}
).

We study basic concepts such as dualizability and adjointability of 1-morphisms.

One of the observations that will play a key role in this paper is that the 1-morphism

{\mathbf{ind}}_{P}:M\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\to G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}

is 2-adjointable, see Sect.
0.5.1
.

0.8.3.

In Sect.
3
we perform the basic trace calculations in
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
and for
1-morphisms

G_{1}\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}\to G_{2}\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}.

In particular, we prove the calculations described in Sect.
0.2
.

0.8.4.

In Sect.
4
we introduce the notion of monodromic action an algebraic group
on an object of
\operatorname{AGCat}
. The actual case of interest is when the group in question
is a torus, but we do this for any algebraic group, just because this is a natural generality.

We start by introducing the subcategory

\operatorname{Shv}(G)^{\operatorname{mon}}\subset\operatorname{Shv}(G),

which is the subcategory generated by 1-dimensional character sheaves. Having this definition,
one can define the monodromic subcategory

\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})^{\operatorname{mon}}\subset\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})

for any prestack
{\mathcal{Y}}
acted on by
G
. This, in turn, leads to the definition of

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G)^{\operatorname{mon}}

which is a
Hopf
algebra object in
\operatorname{AGCat}
. That said, the object
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G)^{\operatorname{mon}}

has the property that the canonical map

{\mathbf{i}}(\operatorname{Shv}(G)^{\operatorname{mon}})\to\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G)^{\operatorname{mon}}

is an isomorphism.

By definition,

(0.27)

G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{mon}}\subset G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}

is a full subcategory, consisting of objects, on which the co-action of
\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G)
factors through

\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G)^{\operatorname{mon}}
. We show that the embedding (
0.27
) has very favorable
properties (for example, it is 2-adjointable).

We also introduce a related notion:

\operatorname{Shv}(G)^{\operatorname{q-mon}}\subset\operatorname{Shv}(G),

and its descendants

\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}})^{\operatorname{q-mon}}\subset\operatorname{Shv}({\mathcal{Y}}),\,\,\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G)^{\operatorname{q-mon}}\subset\underline{\operatorname{Shv}}(G),\,\,G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{q-mon}}\subset G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}

(when
G
is a torus, there is no difference between the two).

The reason for discussing the quasi-monodromic versions is the following. They provide an answer to the following
question: do there exists objects in
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
, whose underlying object of
\operatorname{AGCat}
lies in the essential image
of
{\mathbf{i}}:\operatorname{DGCat}\to\operatorname{AGCat}
? The answer is that such objects exactly correspond to quasi-monodromic actions.

0.8.5.

In Sect.
5
, we introduce the subcategories

G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{restr}}\subset G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}^{\operatorname{spec.fin}}\subset G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}

and establish the properties described in Sects.
0.3
and
0.6
above.

0.8.6.

In Sect.
6
we review the theory of character sheaves, and then recast from the perspective
of
G\operatorname{-}\mathbf{mod}
.

0.8.7.

In Sect.
7
we prove the transitivity property of the functional equation, mentioned in
Sect.
0.4.3
.

0.8.8.

Finally, in Sect.
8
we assemble all the ingredients and prove the independence of
Deligne-Lusztig characters of the parabolic, as outlined in Sect.
0.4
.

0.8.9.

In Sect.
A
we supply the proofs for some technical statements from Sect.
4
.

0.9.
Conventions and notation

0.9.1.

Our conventions and notations follow those adopted in
[
GRV
]
.

0.9.2.
Category theory

We will be working in the context of higher category theory. When we say “category”
we always mean an
\infty
-category.

We let
\operatorname{Spc}
denote the category of
spaces
, a.k.a.
groupoids
.

Given a category
{\mathbf{C}}
, and
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}
, we let

\operatorname{Maps}_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})\in\operatorname{Spc}

denote the corresponding space of morphisms.

For a category
{\mathbf{C}}
, we let
{\mathbf{C}}^{\operatorname{grpd}}
denote the space of its objects
(i.e., the groupoid obtained from
{\mathbf{C}}
by discarding non-invertible morphisms).

0.9.3.

We will work also with
(\infty,2)
and
(\infty,3)
-categories. We adopt the inductive definition:

Having defined the (symmetric) monoidal
(\infty,1)
-category
n\operatorname{-Cat}

of
(\infty,n)
-categories, we define the (symmetric) monoidal
(\infty,1)
-category
(n+1)\operatorname{-Cat}

to consist of
(\infty,1)
-categories enriched over
n\operatorname{-Cat}
. One then shows that

n\operatorname{-Cat}
itself upgrades to an object of
(n+1)\operatorname{-Cat}
.

Given
{\mathbf{C}}\in n\operatorname{-Cat}
and
n^{\prime}\leq n
, we let

{\mathbf{C}}^{n^{\prime}\operatorname{-Cat}}\in n^{\prime}\operatorname{-Cat}

be the object obtained by discarding non-invertible
n^{\prime\prime}
-morphisms for
n^{\prime}<n^{\prime\prime}\leq n
.

Following the same conventions as in Sect.
0.9.2
, in most cases, we will simply say
“
n
-category” when we mean an
(\infty,n)
-category.

0.9.4.

For an
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathfrak{O}
and
{\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}\in\mathfrak{O}
, we let

{\mathbf{Maps}}_{\mathfrak{O}}({\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2})

the resulting
(\infty,1)
-
category
of morphisms, so that

{\mathbf{Maps}}_{\mathfrak{O}}({\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2})^{\operatorname{grpd}}\simeq\operatorname{Maps}_{\mathfrak{O}}({\mathbf{C}}_{1},{\mathbf{C}}_{2}).

For an
(\infty,3)
-category
{\mathbb{E}}
and
\mathfrak{O}_{1},\mathfrak{O}_{2}\in{\mathbb{E}}
, we let

{\mathbb{M}}aps_{{\mathbb{E}}}(\mathfrak{O}_{1},\mathfrak{O}_{2})

the resulting
(\infty,2)
-
category
of morphisms, so that

{\mathbb{M}}aps_{{\mathbb{E}}}(\mathfrak{O}_{1},\mathfrak{O}_{2})^{1\operatorname{-Cat}}\simeq{\mathbf{Maps}}_{{\mathbb{E}}}(\mathfrak{O}_{1},\mathfrak{O}_{2}).

0.9.5.
An identity crisis

When writing this paper we faced a certain dilemma regarding the usage of symbols
\operatorname{Id}
vs
\operatorname{id}
.
To the extent possible, we have tried to adhere to the following conventions:

For an object
{\mathbf{c}}
in an
(\infty,1)
-category
{\mathbf{C}}
, we denote by
\operatorname{id}_{\mathbf{c}}\in\operatorname{Maps}_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{c}},{\mathbf{c}})

its identity endomorphism. And we denote by
\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{C}}
the identity endofunctor of
{\mathbf{C}}
.

More generally, for a
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathfrak{O}
, and an object
{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathfrak{O}
we denote by
\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{C}}
the identity
1-morphism from
{\mathbf{C}}
to itself. For a 1-morphism
\phi:{\mathbf{C}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{C}}_{2}
in
\mathfrak{O}
, we let

\operatorname{id}_{\phi}
denote the identity 2-endomorphism of
\phi
.

Now, this runs into a clash when we are dealing with an ambient
(\infty,3)
-category
{\mathbb{E}}
. For a pair of
objects
\mathfrak{O}_{1},\mathfrak{O}_{2}\in{\mathbb{E}}
and a 1-morphism
\Phi:\mathfrak{O}_{1}\to\mathfrak{O}_{2}
we will sometimes denote the identity
endomorphism of
\Phi
by
\operatorname{id}_{\Phi}
and sometimes by
\operatorname{Id}_{\Phi}
, depending on the context:

We will use the former, when we want to
de-emphasize
the 3-categorical aspect of
{\mathbb{E}}

(i.e., when for the local discussion, non-invertible 3-morphisms do not play a big role). And we will use the latter
when
\mathfrak{O}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{O}_{2}
are fixed, and our focus is the
(\infty,2)
-category
{\mathbb{M}}aps_{\mathbb{E}}(\mathfrak{O}_{1},\mathfrak{O}_{2})
,
in which
\Phi
is an object.

0.9.6.
Higher algebra

We will do
higher algebra
in vector spaces over the field of coefficients, which
in this paper we take to be
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
.

We let
\operatorname{DGCat}
denote the
(\infty,2)
-category of
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
-linear DG categories,
defined as in
[
GR
, Chapter 1, Sect. 10]
.

We view
\operatorname{DGCat}
as equipped with the symmetric monoidal structure, given by the
Lurie tensor product. The unit object for this structure is
\operatorname{Vect}
,
the category of (chain complexes)
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
-vector spaces.

For a DG category
{\mathbf{C}}
and
{\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2}\in{\mathbf{C}}
, we let

{\mathop{\operatorname{{\mathcal{H}}\it om}}}_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})\in\operatorname{Vect}

denote the corresponding object. We have

\operatorname{Maps}_{{\mathbf{C}}}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})=\tau^{\leq 0}({\mathop{\operatorname{{\mathcal{H}}\it om}}}_{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{c}}_{1},{\mathbf{c}}_{2})),

where we view the right-hand side as a connective spectrum.

0.9.7.
Algebraic geometry

Throughout this paper we will be working with the category of (classical, separated) schemes of finite type
over a ground field
k
, assumed algebraically closed. We denote this category by
\operatorname{Sch}
.
We will not need derived algebraic geometry over
k
for this paper.

Whenever we talk about the Frobenius endomorphism, we will assume that
k=\overline{{\mathbb{F}}}_{q}
, but the scheme

X
in question is defined over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, so that the geometric Frobenius
{\operatorname{Frob}}_{X}
on
X
is defined.

By a prestack we shall mean a (classical) prestack locally of finite type over
k

(see
[
GR
, Chapter 2, Sect. 1.3.6]
for what this means). We denote the corresponding category
by
\operatorname{PreStk}
.

0.9.8.

We will work with the sheaf theory of
\ell
-adic sheaves

X\mapsto\operatorname{Shv}(X),\quad X\in\operatorname{Sch}

as defined in
[
AGKRRV1
, Sect. 1.1]
.

The above assignment extends to prestacks by the procedure of right Kan extension.

0.9.9.
Groups

We let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
k
. For most of the paper, we will assume that
G

is is connected and reductive.

0.10.
Acknowledgements

We dedicate this paper to G. Lusztig for creating this area of mathematics.

We wish to thank R. Bezrukavnikov, C. Chan, G. Dhillon, A. Eteve, K. Lin, S. Raskin, W. Reeves and P. Scholze
for some very helpful discussions.

The research of Y.V. was partially supported by the ISF grants 2091/21 and 2889/25.

1.
Higher categorical trace

The usual operation of trace takes a (dualizable!) object
{\mathbf{o}}
in a symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{O}}
,
and attaches to an endomorphism
F
of
{\mathbf{O}}
an endomorphism
\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{o}})
of
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}
. Now,
when
{\mathbf{O}}
is a symmetric monoidal 2-category
{\mathbf{O}}
, the trace operation interacts in a non-trivial
way with 1-morphisms in
{\mathbf{O}}
. And this interaction becomes significantly richer when
{\mathbf{C}}
is a 3-category.

We will describe these interactions in the present section.

1.1.
Trace in 1-categories

1.1.1.

Let
{\mathbf{O}}
be a symmetric monoidal category. Let
{\mathbf{L}}({\mathbf{O}})
denote the (symmetric monoidal) groupoid
consisting of pairs
({\mathbf{O}},F)
, where
{\mathbf{o}}
is a
dualizable
object of
{\mathbf{O}}

and
F
is its endomorphism.

The trace construction is a (symmetric monoidal) functor

(1.1)

\operatorname{Tr}:{\mathbf{L}}({\mathbf{O}})\to{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm End}}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}})=:\Omega({\mathbf{O}}).

1.1.2.

Explicitly, (
1.1
) sends
F\in{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm End}}}({\mathbf{o}})
to the composition

(1.2)

{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}\overset{\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}\overset{F\otimes\operatorname{id}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}\overset{\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}.

1.1.3.

Here are the basic properties of the trace construction:

•

Trace is invariant under duality, i.e.,

(1.3)

\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{o}})\simeq\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\vee},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}),

where
F^{\vee}
denotes the dual morphism. Indeed,

\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{o}})=\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}\circ(F\otimes\operatorname{id})(\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}})\simeq\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes F^{\vee})(\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}})=\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\vee},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee});

•

Trace is cyclic, i.e., for
{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\overset{F_{1,2}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
and
{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\overset{F_{2,1}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{1}
, we have

\operatorname{Tr}({\mathbf{o}}_{1},F_{2,1}\circ F_{1,2})\simeq\operatorname{Tr}({\mathbf{o}}_{2},F_{1,2}\circ F_{2,1}).

Indeed, both sides are obtained by applying
\operatorname{ev}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}\otimes\operatorname{ev}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
to

\sigma_{1,3}\left((F_{1,2}\otimes\operatorname{id})(\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}})\otimes(F_{2,1}\otimes\operatorname{id})(\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}})\right)\in\operatorname{Maps}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}},{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{1}^{\vee}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{2}^{\vee}),

where
\sigma_{1,3}
is the transposition of factors.

1.2.
Trace in 2-categories

1.2.1.

Let now
{\mathbf{O}}
be a 2-category. In this case, we will view
\Omega({\mathbf{O}})
as a
category

(rather than groupoid).

Let
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
be the category whose objects are

(1.4)

{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\overset{t}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2},

and whose morphisms are commutative diagrams

(1.5)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2}.}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

If
{\mathbf{O}}
is symmetric monoidal, then
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
also acquires a symmetric monoidal structure.

1.2.2.

Note that in a 2-category, we can talk about a 1-morphism being adjointable
(here and in the sequel, unless specified otherwise, by “adjointable” we will mean
“admits a right adjoint”).

Lemma 1.2.3
.

An object (
1.4
) is dualizable if and only if
{\mathbf{o}}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
are dualizable
as objects of
{\mathbf{O}}^{1\operatorname{-Cat}}
and
t
is adjointable. Moreover, in this case, the dual of (
1.4
)
is given by

{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{1}\overset{(t^{R})^{\vee}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2}^{\vee}.

Proof.

We will prove the (slightly less tautological) “only” if direction.
The functors

\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})\to{\mathbf{O}}^{1\operatorname{-Cat}}

that send (
1.4
) to
{\mathbf{o}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
, respectively, are symmetric monoidal.
Hence, if (
1.4
) is dualizable, then so are
{\mathbf{o}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
, and the dual has
the shape

{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{1}\overset{s}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{2},

for some 1-morphism
s
. We will show that
s^{\vee}:{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\to{\mathbf{o}}_{1}
is a right adjoint of
t
.

Indeed, consider the unit of the duality between
{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\overset{t}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
and
{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{1}\overset{s}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{2}
;
it is represented by a diagram

\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{1}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{2}^{\vee}.}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{t\otimes s}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

The 2-morphism
\alpha
is thus a map

(t\otimes s)\circ\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}\to\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}.

Rewriting

(t\otimes s)\circ\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}\simeq((t\circ s^{\vee})\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{2}})\circ\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}},

we obtain a map

((t\circ s^{\vee})\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{2}})\circ\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}\to\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}},

or, equivalently, a map

t\circ s^{\vee}\to\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}.

Similarly, by considering the counit of the adjunction,
one obtains a map

\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}\to s^{\vee}\otimes t,

and one readily checks that these two maps satisfy the adjunction axioms.

∎

1.2.4.

Note that

\Omega({\mathbf{1}}_{\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})})\simeq\operatorname{Funct}([0,1],\Omega({\mathbf{O}}))^{\operatorname{grpd}}.

In particular, applying the trace construction from Sect.
1.1.1
to
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
and using
Lemma
1.2.3
, we obtain the following construction:

Let us be given a diagram

(1.6)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2},}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

where
{\mathbf{o}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
are dualizable as objects of
{\mathbf{O}}^{1\operatorname{-Cat}}
, and
t
is adjointable.
Then the 2-morphism
\alpha
induces a map

(1.7)

\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha,t):\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{1})\to\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})

in the
category

\Omega({\mathbf{O}})
.

1.2.5.

Explicitly,
\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha,t)
is the 2-morphism equal to the composition

(1.8)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{1}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{2}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{1}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}_{2}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{t\otimes(t^{R})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{t\otimes(t^{R})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha\otimes\operatorname{Id}}

1.2.6.

Equivalently,
\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha,t)
is the following composition

(1.9)

\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{1})\overset{\text{unit of the adj}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1}\circ t^{R}\circ t,{\mathbf{o}}_{1})\overset{\text{cyclicity of trace}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Tr}(t\circ F_{1}\circ t^{R},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})\overset{\alpha}{\to}\\
\to\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}\circ t\circ t^{R},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})\overset{\text{counit of the adj}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2},{\mathbf{o}}_{2}),

where the arrows are induced by the (obvious) map

\operatorname{Maps}_{{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm End}}}({\mathbf{o}})}(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})\to\operatorname{Maps}_{{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm End}}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}})}(\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\prime},{\mathbf{O}}),\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\prime\prime},{\mathbf{O}})),

for a dualizable object
{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}
and
F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathop{\operatorname{\rm End}}}({\mathbf{o}})
.

1.2.7.

A particular case of the above construction is when
{\mathbf{o}}_{1}={\mathbf{o}}={\mathbf{o}}_{2}
,
t=\operatorname{Id}
, and
\alpha
amounts to a 2-morphism

F_{1}\overset{\alpha}{\Rightarrow}F_{2}.

In this case
\alpha
induces a map

\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}})\overset{\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha,\operatorname{Id})}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2},{\mathbf{o}}).

1.2.8.

Given a diagram (
1.6
), consider the diagram

(1.10)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{2},}
\scriptstyle{F^{\vee}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{(t^{R})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{(t^{R})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{F^{\vee}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}

where

(t^{R})^{\vee}\circ F_{1}^{\vee}\overset{\alpha^{\prime}}{\to}F_{2}^{\vee}\circ(t^{R})^{\vee}

is obtained by duality from

F_{1}\circ t^{R}\overset{\alpha^{\operatorname{BC}}}{\to}t^{R}\circ F_{2},

where
\alpha^{\operatorname{BC}}
is obtained
2
2

2

Here and in the sequel, “BC” stands for Beck-Chevalley.

from
\alpha
by adjunction.

Note that
(t^{R})^{\vee}
is adjointable, namely, its right adjoint is
t^{\vee}
. So the map

\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\vee}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{1})\overset{\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha^{\prime},(t^{R})^{\vee})}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\vee}_{2},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{2})

is defined.

Unwinding the constructions, we obtain that the diagram

\begin{CD}\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}}_{1})@>{\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha,t)}>{}>\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2},{\mathbf{o}}_{2})\\
@V{\text{\eqref{e:Tr of dual}}}V{\sim}V@V{\text{\eqref{e:Tr of dual}}}V{}V\\
\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\vee}_{1},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{1})@>{\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha^{\prime},(t^{R})^{\vee})}>{}>\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\vee}_{2},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}_{2})\end{CD}

commutes.

1.2.9.

More generally, for an index category
I
, we can consider the (symmetric monoidal) 1-category

\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{right-lax}}(I,{\mathbf{O}})

and a symmetric functor

(1.11)

{\mathbf{L}}(\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{right-lax}}(I,{\mathbf{O}}))\overset{\operatorname{Tr}}{\to}\Omega({\mathbf{1}}_{\operatorname{Funct}_{\operatorname{right-lax}}(I,{\mathbf{O}})})\simeq\operatorname{Funct}(I,\Omega({\mathbf{O}}))^{\operatorname{grpd}}.

Furthermore, the functors (
1.11
) are functorial with respect to
I
, in the sense that
we obtain a natural transformation between two functors

\operatorname{Cat}\to\operatorname{Grpd}^{\operatorname{SymMon}}.

1.2.10.

Let
{\mathbf{L}}({\mathbf{O}})
be the symmetric monoidal category, whose underlying groupoid is
{\mathbf{L}}({\mathbf{O}}^{1-\operatorname{Cat}})
, and
where 1-morphisms are given by diagrams (
1.6
) with
t
adjointable.

The functoriality of (
1.11
) with respect to the index category implies that the trace construction
extends to a symmetric monoidal functor

(1.12)

{\mathbf{L}}({\mathbf{O}})\to\Omega({\mathbf{O}}).

1.3.
The notion of 2-dualizability

1.3.1.

Let
{\mathbf{O}}
be a symmetric monoidal 2-category. Recall that an object
{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}
is said to be 2-dualizable if
the following conditions hold:

•

{\mathbf{o}}
is dualizable as an object of
{\mathbf{O}}^{1\operatorname{-Cat}}
;

•

The unit and counit 1-morphisms

\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}}:{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}\to{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}\text{ and }\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}:{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}\to{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}

are adjointable.

1.3.2.
Example

Let
{\mathbf{O}}=\operatorname{DGCat}
, so that
{\mathbf{o}}
is a DG category
{\mathbf{C}}
. Assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
is compactly generated
(in particular, it is dualizable as an object of
\operatorname{DGCat}^{1\operatorname{-Cat}}
).

Then
{\mathbf{C}}
is 2-dualizable if and
only if it is
smooth
and
proper
.

Remark 1.3.3
.

Passing to the right adjoints of the unit and counit of the duality between
{\mathbf{o}}
and
{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}
, we obtain a

different
datum of duality. The discrepancy between the two is an automorphism of
{\mathbf{o}}
, called

Serre functor
, and denoted
\operatorname{Se}_{\mathbf{o}}
.

1.3.4.

Let
{\mathbf{O}}
be as above, and let
{\mathbf{o}}\in{\mathbf{O}}
be a 2-dualizable object. Let
F
be an adjointable 1-endomorphsim of

{\mathbf{o}}
. We have:

Lemma 1.3.5
.

Under the above circumstances, the object
\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{o}})\in\Omega({\mathbf{O}})
is dualizable with dual

\operatorname{Tr}(F^{R},{\mathbf{o}})\simeq\operatorname{Tr}((F^{R})^{\vee},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}).

Remark 1.3.6
.

One can show that if
{\mathbf{o}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
are 2-dualizable, and
t:{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\to{\mathbf{o}}_{2}
admits a right adjoint,
then
t^{R}
is also admits a right adjoint. In fact,

(t^{R})^{R}\simeq\operatorname{Se}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}\circ t\circ\operatorname{Se}^{-1}_{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}.

Proof of Lemma
1.3.5
.

We rewrite
\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{o}})
as
\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\vee},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee})
. Now the assertion of the lemma
follows the fact that the functor

\operatorname{Tr}:{\mathbf{L}}({\mathbf{O}})\to\Omega({\mathbf{O}})

is symmetric monoidal and
compatible with compositions of adjointable 1-morphisms (see (
1.12
)), so that the duality data for
{\mathbf{o}}
and
{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}

induces one on
\operatorname{Tr}(F^{R},{\mathbf{o}})
and
\operatorname{Tr}(F^{\vee},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee})
via
the diagrams

\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}}}
\scriptstyle{F^{R}\otimes F^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{1}}

and

\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}},}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}}
\scriptstyle{F^{R}\otimes F^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{2}}

respectively, where:

•

\alpha_{1}
is the 2-morphism

\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}}\to((F^{R}\circ F)\otimes\operatorname{Id})(\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}})\simeq(F^{R}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(F\otimes\operatorname{Id})(\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}})\simeq\\
\simeq(F^{R}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes F^{\vee})(\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}})\simeq(F^{R}\otimes F^{\vee})(\operatorname{u}_{\mathbf{o}});

•

\alpha_{2}
is the 2-morphism

\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}\circ(F^{R}\otimes F^{\vee})\simeq\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}\circ((F\circ F^{R})\otimes\operatorname{Id})\to\operatorname{ev}_{\mathbf{o}}.

∎

1.3.7.

Let
{\mathbf{o}}
be again a 2-dualizable object, and let
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
be two adjointable endomorphisms of
{\mathbf{o}}

that (lax) commute with each other, i.e., we are given a
2
-morphism

F_{2}\circ F_{1}\overset{\beta}{\to}F_{1}\circ F_{2}.

By adjunction, this also gives a
2
-morphism

F_{1}\circ F_{2}^{R}\overset{\beta^{\operatorname{BC}}}{\to}F_{2}^{R}\circ F_{1},

where the superscript
\operatorname{BC}
stands for Beck-Chevalley, see Sect.
1.2.8
.

The construction (
1.7
) defines maps

(1.13)

\operatorname{Tr}(\beta,F_{2}):\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}})\to\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}})\text{ and }\operatorname{Tr}(\beta^{\operatorname{BC}},F_{1}):\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{R},{\mathbf{o}})\to\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{R},{\mathbf{o}})

in the category
\Omega({\mathbf{O}})
.

By Lemma
1.3.5
, the objects

\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}})\text{ and }\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{R},{\mathbf{o}})

of
\Omega({\mathbf{O}})
are dualizable, so it makes sense to consider the objects

(1.14)

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Tr}(\beta,F_{2}),\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}}))\text{ and }\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Tr}(\beta^{\operatorname{BC}},F_{1}),\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{R},{\mathbf{o}}))

in

\Omega^{2}({\mathbf{O}}):=\Omega(\Omega({\mathbf{O}})).

We have the following fundamental result:

Theorem 1.3.8

(
[
BN1
]
)
.

The objects (
1.14
) are canonically isomorphic.

Proof.

Consider the diagram defining the morphism
\operatorname{Tr}(\beta,F_{2})
:

(1.15)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}\otimes(F_{2}^{R})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}\otimes(F_{2}^{R})^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{{\mathbf{o}}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{{\mathbf{o}}}}
\scriptstyle{\beta\otimes\operatorname{Id}}

The outer square of this diagram is

(1.16)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}(\beta,F_{2})}

Now, we perform the following maneuver on diagram (
1.15
): first pass to right adjoints along the vertical arrows and then
we flip the resulting diagram around the horizontal axis. This produces the following diagram:

(1.17)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}\otimes{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathbf{O}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}^{R}\otimes F_{2}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}^{R}\otimes F_{2}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{u}_{{\mathbf{o}}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{{\mathbf{o}}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{ev}_{{\mathbf{o}}}}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\operatorname{BC}}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}

where the leftmost and rightmost 2-morphisms are the ones in Lemma
1.3.5
defining the duality between
\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{R},{\mathbf{o}})
and
\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{\vee},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee})
.

By the hypothesis on
{\mathbf{o}}
and
F_{1},F_{2}
, we can read the diagram (
1.17
) from left to right as a sequence of 3 composable
1-morphisms in
{\mathbf{L}}({\mathbf{O}})
.

On the one hand, applying the trace functor (
1.12
) to this sequence, we obtain

(1.18)

{\mathbf{1}}_{\Omega({\mathbf{O}})}\overset{\operatorname{u}_{\operatorname{Tr}((F_{2})^{R},{\mathbf{o}})}}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Tr}((F_{2})^{R},{\mathbf{o}})\otimes\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{\vee},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee})\overset{\operatorname{Tr}(\beta^{\operatorname{BC}},F_{1})\otimes\operatorname{Id}}{\longrightarrow}\\
\to\operatorname{Tr}((F_{2})^{R},{\mathbf{o}})\otimes\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{\vee},{\mathbf{o}}^{\vee})\overset{\operatorname{ev}_{\operatorname{Tr}((F_{2})^{R},{\mathbf{o}})}}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbf{1}}_{\Omega({\mathbf{O}})},

whose composite is by definition

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Tr}(\beta^{\operatorname{BC}},F_{1}),\operatorname{Tr}(F_{2}^{R},{\mathbf{o}}))\in\Omega^{2}({\mathbf{O}}).

On the other hand, the outer square of (
1.17
) is obtained from (
1.16
) by passing to right adjoints along the vertical morphisms and flipping the resulting diagram around the horizontal axis, which gives

(1.19)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{1}}_{{\mathbf{O}}}.}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}(\beta,F_{2})}

Now, applying the trace functor (
1.12
) to (
1.19
), we obtain

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Tr}(\beta,F_{2}),\operatorname{Tr}(F_{1},{\mathbf{o}}))\in\Omega^{2}({\mathbf{O}}),

giving the desired isomorphism.

∎

1.3.9.

Suppose now that in the setting of Sect.
1.3.7
,
F_{2}=\operatorname{Id}
and
\beta
is the identity endomorphism of
F_{1}
.
Note that in this case
\beta^{\operatorname{BC}}
is also the identity endomorphism of
F_{1}
.

In this case Theorem
1.3.8
implies:

Corollary 1.3.10
.

Let
F
be an adjointable endomorphism of a 2-dualizable object.
We have a canonical isomorphism

(1.20)

\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{o}}))\simeq\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{id},F),\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathbf{o}})).

1.3.11.
Example

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a smooth and proper category, and let
F
be its endofunctor that preserves
compactness. From Lemma
1.3.5
,
we obtain that
\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{C}})\in\operatorname{Vect}
is dualizable, i.e., finite-dimensional.

From (
1.3.8
) we obtain

\dim(\operatorname{Tr}(F,{\mathbf{C}}))=\operatorname{Tr}(F,\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id},{\mathbf{C}}))

as elements of
\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
.

1.4.
The category of arrows for a 3-category

We now increase the level of complexity and start tackling the 3-categorical situation.

1.4.1.

Let now
{\mathbf{O}}
be a 3-category. In this case, we will perceive
\Omega({\mathbf{O}})
as a 2-category.

Let
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
be the following 2-category. We let

(\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}}))^{1-\operatorname{Cat}}:=\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}}^{2-\operatorname{Cat}}).

For a pair of objects

{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\overset{t}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\text{ and }{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}\overset{t^{\prime}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2}

and a pair of morphisms between them

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

and

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{F}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{F}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\alpha}}

a 2-morphism

(F_{1},F_{2},\alpha)\Rightarrow(\widetilde{F}_{1},\widetilde{F}_{2},\widetilde{\alpha})

is the datum of

(\beta_{1}:F_{1}\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}_{1},\,\,\beta_{2}:F_{2}\Rightarrow\widetilde{F}_{2},\,\,\gamma),

where
\gamma
is a 3-morphism
3
3

3

Note the direction of the arrow
\gamma
!

\widetilde{\alpha}\circ t^{\prime}(\beta_{1})\Rrightarrow\beta_{2}(t)\circ\alpha,

i.e., a 2-morphism in the diagram

\textstyle{t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}}
\textstyle{t^{\prime}\circ\widetilde{F}_{1}}
\textstyle{F_{2}\circ t}
\textstyle{\widetilde{F}_{2}\circ t.}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}(\beta_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{2}(t)}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}

1.4.2.

We observe:

Lemma 1.4.3
.

A 1-morphism

(1.21)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

in
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
is adjointable if and only if the following conditions hold:

•

F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are adjointable in
{\mathbf{O}}^{1\operatorname{-Cat}}
;

•

The 2-morphism
\alpha
is adjointable.

In this case, the right adjoint of (
1.21
) is given by

(1.22)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2},}
\scriptstyle{F^{R}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{F^{R}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{(\alpha^{R})^{\operatorname{BC}}}

where
(-)^{\operatorname{BC}}
is as in Sect.
1.2.8
.

Proof.

We will prove the more difficult direction, i.e., that if the conditions of the lemma hold, then then
(
1.21
) is adjointable. We will construct the unit of the adjunction. The counit is constructed similarly.

Consider the composite 1-morphisms in
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
:

(1.23)

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2},}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{F^{R}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{F^{R}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\alpha}}

as a 1-morphism in
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
.

We need to construct a 2-morphism to (
1.23
) from

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}}

The 2-morphisms
\operatorname{u}_{F_{i}}

\operatorname{Id}\to F^{R}_{1}\circ F_{1}\text{ and }\operatorname{Id}\to F^{R}_{2}\circ F_{2}

are the units of the
(F_{i},F_{i}^{R})
-adjunctions.

It remains to supply a 3-morphism
from the composition

(1.24)

t\overset{\operatorname{u}_{F_{1}}}{\longrightarrow}t\circ F_{1}^{R}\circ F_{1}\overset{\operatorname{u}_{F_{2}}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}^{R}\circ F_{2}\circ t\circ F_{1}^{R}\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha^{R}}{\longrightarrow}\\
\to F_{2}^{R}\circ t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\circ F_{1}^{R}\circ F_{1}\overset{\operatorname{ev}_{F_{1}}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}^{R}\circ t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha}{\to}F_{2}^{R}\circ F_{2}\circ t

to

t\overset{\operatorname{u}_{F_{2}}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}^{R}\circ F_{2}\circ t.

We rewrite (
1.24
) as

t\overset{\operatorname{u}_{F_{2}}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}^{R}\circ F_{2}\circ t\overset{\operatorname{u}_{F_{1}}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}^{R}\circ F_{2}\circ t\circ F_{1}^{R}\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha^{R}}{\longrightarrow}\\
\to F_{2}^{R}\circ t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\circ F_{1}^{R}\circ F_{1}\overset{\operatorname{ev}_{F_{1}}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}^{R}\circ t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha}{\to}F_{2}^{R}\circ F_{2}\circ t.

Hence, it suffices to construct a map from

(1.25)

F_{2}\circ t\overset{\operatorname{u}_{F_{1}}}{\longrightarrow}F_{2}\circ t\circ F_{1}^{R}\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha^{R}}{\longrightarrow}t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\circ F_{1}^{R}\circ F_{1}\overset{\operatorname{ev}_{F_{1}}}{\longrightarrow}t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha}{\to}F_{2}\circ t

to the identity endomorphism of
F_{2}\circ t
.

We rewrite (
1.25
) as

F_{2}\circ t\overset{\alpha^{R}}{\longrightarrow}t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\overset{\operatorname{u}_{F_{1}}}{\longrightarrow}t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\circ F_{1}^{R}\circ F_{1}\overset{\operatorname{ev}_{F_{1}}}{\longrightarrow}t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha}{\to}F_{2}\circ t,

which, by the adjunction axiom is the same as

F_{2}\circ t\overset{\alpha^{R}}{\longrightarrow}t^{\prime}\circ F_{1}\overset{\alpha}{\to}F_{2}\circ t,

which maps to the identity endomorphism via the counit of the
(\alpha,\alpha^{R})
-adjunction.

∎

1.5.
Trace in the 3-categorical setting

In this subsection we let
{\mathbf{O}}
be a symmetric monoidal 3-category.

1.5.1.

Let
{\mathbf{O}}
be a symmetric monoidal 3-category. In this case
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
also acquires a symmetric monoidal structure
(as a 2-category).

Note that we have a canonical equivalence

(1.26)

\Omega(\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}}))\simeq\operatorname{Arr}(\Omega({\mathbf{O}}))

as symmetric monoidal 2-categories.

Applying (
1.12
), we obtain a functor

{\mathbf{L}}(\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}}))\overset{\operatorname{Tr}}{\to}\Omega(\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}}))\simeq\operatorname{Arr}(\Omega({\mathbf{O}})),

where we view
{\mathbf{L}}(\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}}))
as a 1-category, see Sect.
1.2.10
.

1.5.2.

In particular, we obtain the following construction. Let us be given two dualizable objects

{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\overset{t}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2}\text{ and }{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}\overset{t^{\prime}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2}^{\prime}

of
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
, equipped with the following data:

•

An
adjointable
1-morphism

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{G_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{G_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\delta}

(i.e., by Lemma
1.4.3
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
are adjointable 1-morphisms and
\delta
is an adjointable 2-morphism);

•

An endomorphism

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}_{2};}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}

•

An endomorphism

\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{2},\;}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{F^{\prime}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\prime}}

•

A 2-morphism

(1.27)

(G_{1},G_{2},\delta)\circ(F_{1},F_{2},\alpha)\to(F^{\prime}_{1},F^{\prime}_{2},\alpha^{\prime})\circ(G_{1},G_{2},\delta)

in
\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}})
.

Then we obtain a map

(1.28)

\operatorname{Tr}((F_{1},F_{2},\alpha),{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\overset{t}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2})\to\operatorname{Tr}((F^{\prime}_{1},F^{\prime}_{2},\alpha^{\prime}),{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}\overset{t^{\prime}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2}^{\prime})

on
\Omega(\operatorname{Arr}({\mathbf{O}}))
.

Let us decipher what the above means.

1.5.3.

First, the data of (
1.27
) amounts to:

•

A 2-morphism
G_{1}\circ F_{1}\overset{\beta_{1}}{\to}F^{\prime}_{1}\circ G_{1}
;

•

A 2-morphism
G_{2}\circ F_{2}\overset{\beta_{2}}{\to}F^{\prime}_{2}\circ G_{2}
;

•

A 3-morphism
\delta\circ\alpha^{\prime}\circ\beta_{1}\overset{\gamma}{\Rightarrow}\beta_{2}\circ\alpha\circ\delta
,
where the two sides are 2-morphisms

t^{\prime}\circ G_{1}\circ F_{1}\to F^{\prime}_{2}\circ G_{2}\circ t.

1.5.4.

Under the identification (
1.26
), the objects

\operatorname{Tr}((F_{1},F_{2},\alpha),{\mathbf{o}}_{1}\overset{t}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2})\text{ and }\operatorname{Tr}((F^{\prime}_{1},F^{\prime}_{2},\alpha^{\prime}),{\mathbf{o}}^{\prime}_{1}\overset{t^{\prime}}{\to}{\mathbf{o}}_{2}^{\prime})

correspond to the objects
